How a New Trump Administration Rule Could Restrict Access to Green Cards—and Confront Immigrants With a Tough Choice
2026-09-18

How a New Trump Administration Rule Could Restrict Access to Green Cards—and Confront Immigrants With a Tough Choice

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump walks on his way to attend a dinner in the Rose Garden of the White House in Washington, D.C. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump’s Administration is rolling out a new rule that experts predict will make it more difficult for immigrants to get green cards if they access public assistance programs, such as Medicaid and food stamps—and could deter those hoping to obtain the status from enrolling in public benefits. The rule, which goes into effect on Friday, updates a policy dating back to the 19th century that the U.S. has long used to prohibit immigrants who are believed to be likely to become a “public charge” from getting green cards. Trump’s Department of Homeland Security (DHS) has argued that the change “is restoring the basic principle that immigrants must be able to support themselves,” adding that it is “reaffirming the requirement of self-reliance, protecting public resources, and ending policies that encouraged dependency on hard-working American taxpayers.” The rule marks the latest move in Trump’s efforts to sharply restrict both illegal and legal immigration. The President enacted a similar change during his first Administration, but the effort was subjected to court challenges and ultimately rescinded by his successor, Joe Biden. The new rule has already been challenged in court by a coalition of more than a dozen states, including New York and California. It’s unclear at this point how the ongoing litigation will affect the rollout of the rule. Here’s what to know about the new rule and how it could affect immigrants. With its new policy, the Trump Administration is implementing a change to what’s known as the “public charge” rule, a policy first established under the Immigration Act of 1882. Since then, one factor that the U.S. government has considered when reviewing green card applications is whether the applicant is likely to become dependent on government assistance. Historically, officials reviewing these types of applications have only taken into account cash benefit programs—for instance, Supplemental Security Income from Social Security—as “public benefits” that could lead to an immigrant being deemed a “public charge” and having their green card application denied. They have usually not deemed non-cash public benefits, such as Medicaid and food stamps, as programs that would disqualify an immigrant from obtaining permanent resident status. During his first term in the White House, Trump tried to expand the programs that could result in an immigrant being deemed a “public charge,” but a federal judge ruled that the policy had to be vacated, saying it had “numerous unexplained flaws” that made it “arbitrary and capricious.” When former President Joe Biden was in office, he officially rescinded Trump’s rule and stipulated that non-cash benefits would not result in an immigrant being considered a “public charge.” In July, the Trump Administration revealed that it was revoking the Biden-era policy and was rolling out a change similar to the one that faced legal challeges during Trump’s first term. Under the new rule, officials reviewing green card applications are able to consider a broader scope of public benefit programs. The Trump Administration has noted that the new “public charge” rule does not apply to some immigrants—for instance, refugees. The guidance, though, is vague—it doesn’t include a full list of the benefits that can be taken into consideration, saying only that immigration officers will be “using good judgment and discretion” and will be “making individualized, fact-specific public charge inadmissibility determinations, based on a totality of the alien’s circumstances.” “Now, the officers who are reviewing and adjudicating these cases have much more discretion in how they make that determination—whether they believe the person is likely to become a public charge,” says Melissa Shepard, the legal services director at the Immigrant Defenders Law Center. “The difference now is that it’s a much broader analysis, whereas before it was a little more limited.” Adriana Cadena, the executive director of the Protecting Immigrant Families Coalition, says she worries that, because the new policy is so broad, it “opens the door for abuses by immigration officials” during the green card application process. “It’s going from having guidance and clarity and understanding to opening the door to any kinds of programs,” she says. Immigration experts say that the new policy could result in more green card applications being rejected, as well as cause many immigrants to delay seeking permanent resident status. Charles Kuck, an immigration lawyer and former national president of the American immigration Lawyers Association, says that he expects there will be “massive numbers of denials” of green card applications as this new policy is rolled out. He adds that he and his colleagues have heard from some of their clients that they plan to wait until after Trump leaves the White House before filing their green card applications. People who do not have permanent resident status in the U.S. are not eligible for many public assistance programs, but experts also warn that the new rule could result in immigrants disenrolling from public benefit programs that they or their children are eligible for because they are afraid of potentially harming their chances of obtaining a green card in the future. Experts are additionally concerned that the Trump Administration’s new rule opens the door to immigration officials taking into account whether a green card applicant’s family member used a public benefit. The new rule stipulates that “if there is evidence in the record the alien’s family members whom the alien is legally obligated to support receive means-tested public benefits based on the alien’s income falling below the designated threshold, DHS would consider that fact.” Kuck says he fears that this could lead to a situation where, for example, if a green card applicant’s child who is a U.S. citizen receives a public benefit, such as a free school lunch program, that could lead to their parent’s application being denied. According to Shepard, it’s not yet clear how exactly the new policy will be applied in practice because it is so broad and gives immigration officers “so much discretion.” But she says she believes the change will cause people to be “a lot more cautious” and potentially fearful to access public assistance programs. Cadena says the new rule is “forcing families to choose between either being able to adjust their status in the future or be able to have access to essential programs like food and child care.” “In the current environment that we are in, where immigrant family safety is constantly being threatened, immigrant families … want to be able to keep their families together,” Cadena says. “And if keeping their child enrolled in an important program like Medicaid is going to threaten that, then they would more than likely disenroll their children from these programs.” DHS has previously estimated that the rollout of the “public charge” change could lead to more than 950,000 people disenrolling from or choosing not to enroll in public assistance programs. The change is the latest action the Trump Administration has taken to restrict immigration, including ramping up efforts to limit several legal pathways to immigration and proposing raising the fees associated with applying for citizenship. “This is just one small factor or small piece of the entire immigration system, I think, that is under attack,” Shepard says.

Why the Outcome of Russia's First Parliamentary Elections in Years Is All But Assured
2026-09-18

Why the Outcome of Russia's First Parliamentary Elections in Years Is All But Assured

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In this pool photograph distributed by the Russian state agency Sputnik, Russia's President Vladimir Putin gives a speech during a ceremony to present state awards in the defence industry sector at the Kremlin in Moscow on September 18, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Russia is just one day into three-day elections that will determine the makeup of the lower house of the country’s parliament, the State Duma. But the outcome of the vote has been all but guaranteed since before the first ballots were cast. The elections, the first parliamentary vote the country has held since its invasion of Ukraine in 2022, are widely expected to result in the Kremlin-aligned United Russia party maintaining its overwhelming dominance in the Duma. The party currently holds 310 of the 450 seats in the chamber, giving it a supermajority and the power to make major legislative decisions without the input of other parties. It needs to keep at least 300 seats to retain that supermajority. Four other political parties are also expected to be represented in the Duma following the election, all of which generally vote in line with United Russia and support the war in Ukraine. They are the Communist Party of the Russian Federation, the Liberal Democratic Party of Russia, the A Just Russia party, and the New People party. Democracy watchdogs and foreign governments have denounced Russia’s elections as being neither free nor fair. Russian officials have rejected such claims in the past, saying that the country carries out its voting process in line with the law. But experts tell TIME that the Russian government exerts its influence in ways that all but predetermine the results of elections and ensure that President Vladimir Putin and United Russia maintain control. And this year’s vote, says David Szakonyi, an expert on Russia and election fraud who is professor at George Washington University, is “occurring in the most repressive political environment in Russia's post-Soviet history.” Here's what experts have to say about how the Kremlin ensures elections result in its desired outcome—and why it continues to hold them. The Kremlin uses multiple levers to influence elections on the government level, at the polls, and in daily civilian life, according to Szakonyi. “Without a doubt, the most powerful tool” in the Kremlin’s arsenal, Szakonyi says, is limiting which candidates appear on the ballot in the first place. In the case of the ongoing parliamentary vote, the one anti-war party that was registered by the Central Election Commission, the Yabloko party, was prohibited from participating in the election by Russia’s Supreme Court. The high court ordered that the party be removed from the ballot last month in response to a lawsuit from the pro-Kremlin Rodina party that accused the Yabloko party, which has garnered support from young Russians and those opposed to the war in Ukraine, of copyright infringement, receiving foreign funding, and extremism. Szakoni says that the Kremlin has the ability to “harness the judicial system to pass rulings” such as the Supreme Court’s decision against the Yabloko party, which it uses as a “legal veneer” that shields the influence of Putin’s government over the ballot. This screening of anti-war and other opposition parties, he says, comes months in advance of elections, creating a list of candidates that includes little or no meaningful opposition at all. The Kremlin has also labeled opposition figures who oppose the war as “foreign agents,” a designation that prevents them from holding public office. In other instances, opposition leaders and activists such as Alexei Navalny have been prosecuted and imprisoned. Still others have fled the country. Experts tell TIME that the Russian government has also exercised its influence more directly on vote counts to achieve its desired election results. “Historically,” Szakonyi says, Russian electoral officials have “intervened regularly to deliver votes to the ruling party and take them away from competitors.” In the past, this was done via “crude ballot box stuffing,” he says; now, he asserts it is also performed by altering the results of online votes. Online voting is used in more than a third of Russia’s regions, which Szakonyi asserts puts more power in the government’s hands since it eliminates the physical tally represented by paper ballots. “The way that the electronic votes are being counted is almost completely a black box for the rest of society,” he says. Regina Smyth, an expert on Russia and authoritarian regimes who is a professor at Indiana University, says that “falsification,” or the practice of altering the number of votes that are counted, occurs at precinct election commissions, of which there are over 90,000, all across Russia, that she says are “mostly staffed by loyalists, and they will simply report false numbers.” There is also fraud, Smyth alleges, in “mobile voting,” when officials deliver and pick up ballots from people in their homes who are, for example, disabled or ill and unable to vote otherwise. On a deeper level, an “implicit threat” stemming from the Russian government sways countless votes each election cycle, Szakonyi contends. “Many Russian voters do not have the autonomy to cast an independent vote. They work for the state,” he says. He argues that many university students, healthcare workers, and teachers, whose livelihoods depend on state-run organizations, belong to a “block of captive, vulnerable voters” who fear prosecution by the Kremlin more than they value the influence of their ballot. Voting is also taking place this year in Russian-controlled areas of Ukraine: in Crimea, which Russia annexed in 2014, and in the Luhansk, Donetsk, Kherson, and Zaporizhzhia regions. “In the occupied territories of Ukraine, where the elections are being held in these illegally occupied territories, soldiers, heavily armed soldiers, travel from house to house coercing people to vote,” Smyth tells TIME. On top of the various methods the Kremlin allegedly uses to achieve the voting results it wants, there are slim to no oversight mechanisms in place to document election interference in the country. Golos, which was the only independent election watchdog within Russia, shuttered in 2025 following years of governmental pressure after its chairman was found guilty of working with an "undesirable organization" and imprisoned in a penal colony. Szakonyi describes Golos’ loss as the “biggest blow toward any type of accountability” for the Kremlin, saying it was “the biggest tool that civil society had to identify, uncover, and publicize the fraud that was basically happening in every single electoral cycle.” Russia has also refused to invite international election monitors from the Organization for Security ​and Co-operation in Europe (OSCE), the world’s largest regional security organization, to observe this year’s elections. Maria Telalian, director of the OSCE’s election-monitoring office, the Office for Democratic Institutions and Human Rights, said in a statement last month that the decision not to invite any OSCE monitors was “deeply regrettable, particularly as it continues a pattern of not implementing the commitments that every OSCE participating State has undertaken towards its own citizens and the international community.” The ongoing vote marks the third nationwide election in Russia to take place without OSCE observation. Russia previously imposed severe limits on OSCE election observation in 2021 that the organization said made monitoring that year’s parliamentary elections in the country impossible. It did not invite the group’s monitors to the 2024 presidential election at all. Despite the results of the parliamentary elections being all but determined, they carry a symbolic weight crucial to Putin’s legitimacy in the public eye and his desire to continue the war against Ukraine, experts tell TIME. “There's lots of reasons to have elections. First and foremost, the Russian population expects elections,” Szakonyi says. “Putin's legitimacy depends on his ability to win election votes to the presidency and have his ruling party win on a regular interval, and demonstrate that they're popular and that their policies are in demand from the citizenry.” During an address in which Putin urged Russians to vote days before the elections began, the leader said that “your choice and resolve will show once again that millions of people stand behind our fighters” and that “participating in the elections is our shared contribution to Russia’s victory.” “This is a way for him to theoretically show that the Russian population is united behind his war and wants to continue fighting, and that Russia has had this referendum and will continue to push harder,” Szakonyi says.

Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Has Nothing New to Say About Female Rage
2026-09-18

Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Has Nothing New to Say About Female Rage

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Ella Beatty in Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings This article discusses the full season of Netflix’s Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story is about female rage and repression. That isn’t my personal interpretation of the latest installment of Ian Brennan and Ryan Murphy’s Netflix true-crime anthology. It’s the talking point that the show’s makers have been leading with, in publicity materials and in multiple interviews. These are fine themes for the first season of Monster with a female subject, even if they’re not exactly unique within a post-Big-Little-Lies, #MeToo-informed TV landscape where so many dramas aimed at women double as amplifiers for gendered frustrations. The well of misogyny isn’t in danger of running dry anytime soon. But if we must keep diving into it, each descent should at least leave us with some new insight or nuance. Lizzie Borden, now streaming, fails to clear even that bar. Perhaps the least historically accurate Monster yet—and that’s saying something—the season uses the infamous tale of a Fall River, Mass. woman who probably hacked her wealthy father and stepmother to death with an axe in 1892 as a conduit to just about every plague on womankind you can imagine, past, present, and perennial. Rape. Domestic violence. Pregnancy and childbirth. Housework. Menstrual maladies. Pressure to marry. The premium put on physical beauty. The perils of sex work. Confinement in the home and subjugation to male relatives. By tying together Lizzie’s story with the legends of supposed forerunner Elizabeth Báthory and descendent Aileen Wuornos, Brennan, who wrote every episode, suggests a simplistic kinship between all female killers. Rebecca Hall, left, and Ella Beatty in Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Suzanne Tenner—Netflix There’s no use fretting over the extremely purposeful anachronisms and inaccuracies in this retelling, which plays as fast and loose with the Borden family as that classic nursery rhyme. With 26-year-old Ella Beatty starring as a dreamy, blank-screen Lizzie, who ages from 32 to her 60s by the end of the season, and Charlie Hunnam and Rebecca Hall cast as parents who were really a couple of decades the actors’ seniors, everyone is much younger and prettier than their real-life counterparts. And more shocking, in some ways, than the hyperspeed double murder reenactment that opens the premiere, is what immediately follows it: a flashback to four months earlier, when Lizzie scares off a suitor by passing a bucket of menstrual blood on the white sofa where they’re sitting. As it turns out, what the fictionalized Bordens refer to as her “lady time” had some relevance to the actual case. But Lizzie Borden is such a massive remix, mingling facts with half-truths and rumors and fabrications, it barely matters which detail is which. Because its source material is not especially extensive—one double homicide, as opposed to the lengthy and lurid sagas of most past Monster subjects—the show is forced to stretch. The series is padded with Fall River horror scenes that either never happened (the oyster poisoning that leads to so much operatic barfing) or didn’t transpire remotely the way they’re shown (the murder of Bertha Manchester, played by Linda Reiter). Brennan also adds queer love to Lizzie’s life, in the form of the family’s maid, Bridget Sullivan, who they really did call Maggie, after a former servant. He is far from the first to investigate this angle; the 2018 film Lizzie, to take just one example, is also built around a romance, between Chloë Sevigny’s Lizzie and Kristen Stewart’s Bridget. Monster is, however, the first dramatization to make such imaginative use of the character. Phantom Thread breakout Vicky Krieps, who played ‘Bitch of Buchenwald’ Ilse Koch in Monster’s Ed Gein season, is a conspicuously Germanic Irish maid, with an ahistorical shag haircut dyed a series of shades reminiscent of, by turns, Wuornos and Ziggy Stardust. She has picked up skills in everything from cooking and cleaning to pharmacology in the course of her global travels. It’s no mystery why the awkward, sheltered Lizzie, who craves a bigger life, is enchanted by this glamorous, androgynous outsider. Maggie is easily the best part of the season, thanks in no small part to Krieps’ at once humane and otherworldly performance. Charlie Hunnam and Vicky Krieps in Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Suzanne Tenner—Netflix She’s also the unstuck-in-time character who first connects Lizzie to murderesses from other eras. As Maggie reads from a French book about Báthory, a 16th century Hungarian noblewoman said to have killed hundreds of young women and bathed in their blood in pursuit of eternal youth, we see Krieps—with hair and makeup straight out of the goth club—as the so-called Blood Countess. The Runaways’ anthem of teen-girl rebellion “Cherry Bomb” blares. And Lizzie is electrified by the story. She feels a kinship with Báthory. Never mind that killing your cruel parents to free yourself and massacring a village’s worth of peasant girls because La Mer has yet to be invented are about as different as murder motives get. The fact that each woman is acting in response to some form of sexism is, I guess, supposed to bridge that gulf. It takes almost half the season for the third member of the deadly trio, played by Murphyverse MVP Sarah Paulson, to show up. Often framed as America’s first female serial killer, Wuornos, a Florida sex worker who killed seven johns between 1989 and 1990, is, in this telling, as inspired by Lizzie as Lizzie is by Báthory. Her presence, and her unsuccessful attempt to claim in court that she acted in self-defense against rape, allows the show to take on—and force us to watch—sexual violence. I’m not sure it needed to be shoehorned into the rare true-crime drama where it doesn’t have an obvious place. Brennan also connects Aileen’s well-documented queerness with the speculative relationship between Lizzie and Maggie. On a pilgrimage to the Borden home, now a bed and breakfast, Aileen and her girlfriend (Jessie Lee) have public sex. A fantasy sequence has the two couples dancing side-by-side in Lizzie’s sacred garden shed. But it’s never clear what Monster is trying to say about lesbians and murder, and this is a case where ambiguity leaves space to insert some pretty dark assumptions. The outcomes for each of the three female killers are as different as their motivations. Lizzie is acquitted and buys a mansion with her inheritance, presumably because sexist men can’t conceive of a woman committing such physical murders—and because she learns to correctly perform femininity, crying and fainting at appropriate moments, during the trial. Despite wrenching testimony, coarse, unfeminine Aileen is convicted and executed. This might lead you to conclude that privilege and falling in line with patriarchal expectations are what it takes for a woman to get away with murder. But then there’s the rich, powerful, and apparently quite appearance-obsessed Báthory. Our last glimpse of the countess is as a rotting corpse, bricked up in her castle for her sins. So what is really being said here? Sarah Paulson, left, and Jessie Lee in Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Netflix Not much, as far as I can tell. Lizzie Borden certainly makes a strong case that women have always been repressed, that rage is the obvious product of that repression, and that liberation is the only antidote. (Of course we get a scene in the finale where Lizzie celebrates suffrage.) But if you didn’t know that, it’s probably also news to you that water is wet and the pope is Catholic. The show’s obsession with women’s bodies as the sites of unending horrors reminds me of House of the Dragon’s repeated depictions of traumatic childbirth. It’s as though viewers are expected to go in completely ignorant of basic hardships afflicting half the human population and must see them reproduced over and over again in gory detail to be enlightened, much less empathetic. And in that case, I suppose it’s also necessary to render interchangeable three notorious women defined by actions rooted in a near-universal form of rage. But theirs are old stories, already told and retold in every conceivable media, and often, effectively, by women. Wuornos was, of course, most famously portrayed by Charlize Theron, in writer-director Patty Jenkins’ career-making, Oscar-winning 2003 film also titled Monster. Isabelle Huppert stars as Báthory in The Blood Countess, a new feature by German experimental cinema legend Ulrike Ottinger, which is earning praise on the festival circuit for its strangeness and sense of humor. Meanwhile, on TV, creator Liz Meriwether just gave us Furious—another story of female rage, female repression, and yes, even a (fictional) female serial killer. While Krieps is mesmerizing in Monster and frequent Murphy production designer Matthew Flood Ferguson has outdone himself with lavishly suffocating Gilded Age interiors, Furious is the superior show because it takes “being a woman is hard” as the given it is rather than as the endpoint of many repetitive episodes. It has dark, lived-in comedy and real stakes, posing the question of what can realistically be done about systemic misogyny and the anger over it that stains women’s lives. “All your books are written by men,” Maggie scolds Lizzie in Monster’s season premiere. When you consider how heavily male the creative team behind Lizzie Borden is (executive producer Max Winkler directed six episodes and Sarah Adina Smith, two), this kind of has to be a self-aware line. But whether it’s intended as a wry apology or a small joke, I couldn’t tell you.

Trump Announces Ban of CNN, Politico and MS Now From White House
2026-09-18

Trump Announces Ban of CNN, Politico and MS Now From White House

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump announced on Friday he was banning CNN, MS NOW, and Politico from the White House, setting up a new test of the First Amendment’s protection of a free press as his Administration moves to punish news organizations whose coverage he dislikes. In a post on Truth Social, Trump accused the three outlets of repeatedly publishing “FAKE NEWS” and warned that other news organizations could face similar restrictions. “Effective immediately, I am banning Fake News CNN, MSNOW... and Politico... from the White House as a result of their constant ‘reporting’ FAKE NEWS!” Trump wrote. “Media Outlets shouldn’t be able to constantly write or report FICTION and LIES when they’re covering the President of the United States, the Trump Administration, or the United States of America.” Taking questions from reporters in the Oval Office later, Trump said there were no particular stories that prompted the decision. “It's really just cumulative stories over the last few years, you get sick of it,” he said. “They purposely write negative news, and they do that because they want to try and diminish the Republicans and a Republican administration.” It was not immediately clear whether the White House would enforce the prohibition. Despite Trump’s announcement that the ban was effective immediately, CNN reported that its team remained at the White House and continued to cover the Administration on Friday afternoon. No evictions had been observed in the press briefing room as of 5 p.m., although the mood among reporters there was tense. “CNN stands fully behind our White House team and their fair and accurate reporting,” the network said in a statement. “We have a right under the U.S. Constitution to do that reporting without hindrance or interference from the government. Should the ban which President Trump threatened go ahead, it would be an illegal assault on that fundamental and constitutionally-protected right.” When asked by a reporter what exactly the ban would entail, Trump said, "I don't want them in my office. I don't want them here. I would say the ban would go as far as you can." The White House Press Office did not respond to questions about the ban. Last year, Trump barred reporters from The Associated Press from the Oval Office, Air Force One, and other events with limited space after the news organization declined to update their widely used stylebook to adopt his preferred name, “Gulf of America,” for the body of water long known as the Gulf of Mexico. The Associated Press sued, and the case remains ongoing. The uncertainty could set up another legal confrontation over the boundaries of presidential control over access to the White House and the First Amendment’s protection of freedom of the press. The First Amendment protects freedom of the press, a principle intended to allow news organizations to scrutinize those in power without government retaliation for their reporting. Asked about the likelihood of his latest media ban being struck down in court, Trump responded, “I think it's good to point it out whether it survives or doesn't.” He added, “I don't think that a court should allow fake news to be written day after day after day.” The White House has historically maintained regular access for journalists representing a broad range of news organizations, allowing them to question the President, attend briefings, and document the activities of the executive branch. The press corps also serves a practical function in providing independent accounts of presidential movements and events to the public. CNN, in particular, has been part of the traditional five-network television pool that follows the President and provides ongoing coverage of presidential appearances. Trump has tried before to use access to the White House and presidential events as leverage against news organizations whose coverage he disliked. During his first term, he revoked the White House press credentials of Jim Acosta, then a CNN correspondent, after a contentious exchange during a news conference. CNN challenged the decision in court, and the restriction was overturned. The dispute over access has become part of a broader pattern in Trump’s relationship with the news media. Since returning to office, he has pursued legal action against news organizations including The New York Times, The Wall Street Journal, and the BBC after reports he objected to. The cases against the Times and Journal were subsequently dismissed, while the BBC has sought to have the case dismissed. The Trump Administration has repeatedly called itself the “most transparent Administration in history,” pointing to the President’s frequent gaggles with reporters.
The Atlantic
What AI Anxiety Looks Like in China
2026-09-19

What AI Anxiety Looks Like in China

The Atlantic | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
Anxiety about AI in the United States appears to be getting worse. Prosaic concerns about machines disrupting industries, stealing jobs, and compromising schoolwork are giving way to more harrowing fears about the end of humanity. Even after top AI executives in the U.S. called for slowing down the breakneck pace of development, President Trump dismissed these safety concerns as a “hoax” that would hand China an advantage in the race for AI supremacy. Outside of China, Trump’s assertion may appear well founded. While the U.S. roils over the prospects of AI, China’s leadership looks to be charging ahead. In surveys, Chinese people appear more enthusiastic about AI and its potential benefits than respondents in the U.S., and they are adopting it more quickly. Although Chinese President Xi Jinping has expressed more alarm than Trump about the threats of AI—and has even called for regulation to “ensure that AI is always under human control”—his government has made adopting the technology a national priority. The plan is to have AI in every aspect of China’s economy and society by 2035. The country’s steady release of AI models that are open-source, high-performing, and relatively cheap to build and run has been sending shivers through Silicon Valley and Wall Street. Read: What really happens if China wins the AI race? But for all the concern about China’s advances in AI, the reality is that the country is racked by the same anxieties about the onrush of AI as the rest of the world. Chinese social-media platforms are filled with posts in which workers and professionals express their worries that AI will steal their job and livelihood. Leaders in Beijing seem ever more concerned about the potential political and economic risks of this technological disruption. In the U.S., these tensions are being debated openly and, in theory, will be managed in the courts and on Capitol Hill. In China, where there are no such avenues for people to determine their own fate, leaders must still confront the problems and threats inherent to this technological revolution. This puts the Communist Party in a bind: Advancing AI may well empower China on the world stage, but holding the technology back may be necessary for the party to preserve itself. Xu Peng, a 30-year-old actor, was out of work last year when he got an unexpected break. A director had reached out to cast him in a short-drama series, each episode minutes long, which have become enormously popular in China. Xu traveled to Hengdian, a film-production hub in eastern China, and began landing roles in one or two dramas a month. And then it all stopped. Xu had expected to act in two dramas after the Chinese New Year in February, but never heard from the studio. When he contacted the producer, he got the news: The company had canceled the productions and would be making videos using artificial intelligence instead. “It was as though everything was fine yesterday, and today it is suddenly gone,” Xu said. Xu’s fate is the fear of workers across the country—and around the world. Social-media posters regularly express anxiety about being “distilled,” a term Chinese people use for when an employer uses a worker’s data to train an AI model. In May on Douyin, the Chinese version of TikTok, someone going by the name Nanbei wrote that after leaving a recent job, she learned that her former employer had fed thousands of her chat messages, hundreds of documents, and every one of her work emails into an AI system to create a “digital robot” that took over her responsibilities—and even gave it her name. “The company no longer has to pay me a cent in salary, yet it can keep me there ‘working overtime’ forever,” Nanbei wrote. Another worker, using the handle Xiexiaoya, posted on RedNote, China’s Instagram, in June that the prospect of being distilled kept her awake at night. “I’m afraid my AI double might become more popular than I am,” she wrote. Two years ago, Shen Anyu, a voice actor who earns a living from narrating news commentaries and other short programs, began noticing an alarming number of videos on social-media platforms that seemed to feature his voice without his permission. Shen hired a lawyer earlier this year and filed a lawsuit against a voice-generation platform. The case is still pending, but in the meantime, his career is “losing blood,” he said, as he loses work to AI. Living with the new technology “is inevitable,” he said, but “I’m not yet sure how we should coexist with AI.” Though some Chinese people are embracing AI, many are not ready for the change it brings. Bruce Jiang, a 42-year-old commercial director in Shanghai, loves choosing the right lens and finding the perfect shot, and he didn’t want to outsource these and other aspects of his craft to an algorithm. But after his clients began pressuring him to use the technology—“‘You only have to tell AI what you want, and it produces it like magic,’” he said they insisted—he began using tools such as the video-generator Kling for certain tasks. The experience, he said, has been demoralizing. The old method “was work, and it was a cost, but it was also a kind of pleasure,” Jiang said. “That pleasure is disappearing.” Chinese policy makers at national and local levels have introduced ambitious plans and financial support to rush the country into the AI age. Entrepreneurs across China are adopting AI in the hopes of cutting costs, remaining competitive, and aligning their firms with Beijing’s priorities. In China’s cutthroat society—and especially in sectors where AI is making rapid inroads, such as the entertainment industry—many like Jiang are afraid that they’ll get left behind if they don’t join in. But these swift and radical changes have yielded mixed results. The state-linked China Center for Information Industry Development estimated in June that nearly 70 percent of the country’s small businesses have not moved beyond initial trials of adopting AI in their operations, largely owing to the costs and complexity of integrating the technology. After the Chinese AI lab DeepSeek released a large language model last year, Chinese companies rushed to adopt it. But instead of buying a software subscription (an unpopular practice in China owing to the country’s history of piracy), some businesses opted for what locals call an “all-in-one machine,” or a mini data center with DeepSeek installed, which managers simply plugged into their computer systems. This solution was especially popular among state-owned enterprises and government bureaus looking to appear advanced, but the hardware proved to be an ineffective way to use AI. Buyers soon came to see that the machines weren’t worth the investment, and their sales quickly fell. Earlier this year, plenty of buzz similarly surrounded OpenClaw, a free, open-source, autonomous AI agent. Tech companies hosted popular events to help users install and use the tool, a process that Chinese enthusiasts call “raising a lobster.” At the OpenClaw festival in Beijing in March, the internet-service provider Baidu set up pools with live crustaceans to entertain attendees. Local-government officials encouraged the frenzy by pledging financial aid to start-ups that used OpenClaw. Disillusionment came quickly. Some distraught users took to social media to express their alarm at how the AI agent, which required masses of personal data to operate without detailed instructions, had a habit of going rogue. “I jumped on the bandwagon” expecting to get a “digital employee,” a woman using the social-media handle HotdogWang wrote in March on RedNote. “Instead, it turned out to be both a money pit and a ticking time bomb.” Some reported that their AI helper had deleted emails or made credit-card purchases without permission. One Douyin user, posting under the name Qizaibushidaju, wrote, “I simply installed it because everyone else was doing so,” only to uninstall it 72 hours later owing to security concerns. Although Chinese tech executives are not yet engaged in a public debate over the risks of an algorithm-created calamity—the country’s constraints on free speech may inhibit them from speaking out—some Chinese people do worry about more than just jobs and data. “There are only two options for the future,” a man calling himself Qiangni Yinshou wrote on Douyin. “Either humanity goes extinct, or humans shut AI down.” A user by the name of Buyehou wrote on RedNote that when he engaged a chatbot in a philosophical discussion about humanity’s future, he was told that the options included “either merging with AI or being eliminated.” The man added that “when AI becomes far smarter than humans, has consciousness of its own, and can evolve independently, it will be impossible for humans to control it. There is no turning back now.” Despite their outward show of confidence, China’s leaders plainly don’t know how life works with AI either. But figuring it out is an urgent concern here, given Beijing’s evident faith in AI as a magical solution to the country’s deepening economic and social problems, and as an essential tool in the pursuit of global dominance over the United States. In the U.S., the diffusion of AI is largely a bottom-up phenomenon as companies, investors, and consumers test the risks and benefits of this new technology. In China, where the development and spread of AI is a national mission that is costing the government hundreds of billions of dollars, leaders are eager to impose these advances from above. In theory, China’s autocrats can ram through their agenda with scant regard for public sentiment. If confronted with the kinds of protests against AI data centers that have become prevalent in the U.S., Beijing’s leaders could easily send police to crush them and then cover up the problem in a deluge of propaganda. The Communist Party has already shown its willingness to override public concerns in pursuit of technological advantage. In 2024, trials of autonomously driven taxis in the city of Wuhan sparked concern over how robotaxis were “stealing people’s rice bowls”—but the trials have expanded anyway. Yet China’s leaders have traditionally prized policies that promote stable employment out of fear that mounting joblessness could create unrest and threaten the political order. When the country first embraced markets and opened to trade, its rapidly growing economy and expanding private sector could absorb displaced workers. Now, when China’s sluggish economy already has a jobs problem—nearly a fifth of young workers are unemployed—the advances of AI threaten to put many more people out of work. Read: The race for global domination in AI “In a nominally Communist state, the image of the party elite allying with business interests to displace the human proletariat with a robot class of workers carries certain political risks,” Carl Minzner, a senior fellow at the Council on Foreign Relations, told me. “There is the potential for a lot of disgruntled people.” China’s leaders are showing signs of nervousness. Earlier this year a court in Hangzhou, a technology hub in eastern China, upheld a ruling in favor of a tech worker who had challenged his compensation package after being replaced by AI. In the court’s judgment, the dismissal was unlawful and the worker deserved more money. Although a local-court verdict does not set a national precedent, it appears to be a warning to employers that mass AI layoffs are politically unacceptable. “As employers enjoy the benefits of technology, they must also shoulder corresponding social responsibilities,” the Beijing municipal government wrote last year. The state news agency Xinhua commented in March that “those companies that equate AI with ‘reducing staff’ may seem to lower costs in the short term, but in reality, they lose the core competitiveness of talent accumulation and further erode employee trust.” Pushing companies to invest in new technologies without shedding redundant workers may preserve social order, but it would also limit AI’s benefits. Jeffrey Ding, an expert on AI in China at George Washington University, told me that the country’s leaders face a “balancing act” between political and economic necessity. “AI does have the potential for bringing massive, widespread productivity growth,” he said. But he also noted that the Communist Party prioritizes legitimacy and regime stability. If embracing AI demands “massive displacement and a lot of structural changes, then that’s where the trade-off begins to bite, and they might have to veer toward slowing down AI adoption.” If American AI firms do, in fact, slow the pace of innovation, the Communist Party may see this as an opportunity to race ahead in AI. But China’s leaders, facing their own domestic challenges and preoccupied with their own grip on power, may decide that imposing these innovations on the masses is simply too risky—for their own sake, if not for the sake of humanity. That would probably suit many Chinese people just fine. Xu, the actor, has found work teaching archery and horseback riding—skills he acquired in acting gigs. But he hasn’t given up on his theatrical career. He now plans to produce his own short dramas, partly with the help of AI, though he said that the performers on-screen will all be living, breathing people. AI “will not completely replace human actors,” Xu insisted. At least he can hope. Cao Li in Hong Kong contributed reporting to this story.
The Atlantic
Emily Wilson Can’t Let The Odyssey Go
2026-09-19

Emily Wilson Can’t Let The Odyssey Go

The Atlantic | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
Emily Wilson has become the most famous living translator of the Homeric epics. Her 2017 The Odyssey, the first major translation of the poem into English by a woman, has sold more than 1 million copies. (Her Iliad translation came in 2023.) And this summer, she found herself right at the center of cultural discourse after she drew her sword and published a scathing review of Christopher Nolan’s blockbuster adaptation of the ancient work. She didn’t anticipate that she would become part of a pop-culture controversy, and she was reluctant to revisit the episode when we sat down for an interview at this year’s Atlantic Festival. “I really wanted to like the film,” she said, but “it seems to me that criticism has a responsibility to tell the truth.” Her strong convictions animate a new book of essays, Crossing the Wine-Dark Sea, which dissects translations of ancient work with unsparing rigor. Wilson, who is now working on a second translation of The Odyssey, entertained the idea that there might be something “compulsive” in her art and her approach. Her new essays reveal a translator’s difficult balancing act—between fidelity and originality, and between accessibility and strangeness. In the course of our wide-ranging conversation about the pleasures and blind spots of translation—and, yes, the backlash to her now-notorious movie review—she spoke about the imperative she feels to get that balance exactly right. The following conversation was condensed and edited for clarity. Gal Beckerman: You take a pretty hard line in this book about when a text should be retranslated, but you’re translating The Odyssey again. It’s interesting that you should want to go back in again. Have you learned something new about the text or come to see it in a new way that makes it exciting for you to tear it all up and start it from scratch? Emily Wilson: It’s not tearing it up to do a new one, right? Partly it’s that I’m kind of addicted; I can’t stop doing this. I have several other projects I’m supposed to be doing. I did The Odyssey really prioritizing pacing, because my students would very often show up to class saying, Oh, it was so boring. And I would be like, How can The Odyssey be boring? And then I would realize it’s because the translation was twice as long as the original. Then, with my Iliad translation, I was a little bit looser about not confining myself to the exact same number of lines as the original. I thought there were advantages to doing that. So as soon as I’d finished The Iliad, I wanted to go back to The Odyssey and start all over again to produce one that matches my Iliad. So it’s not that I think there’s anything wrong with my current translation, and I think that there will be readers who will like that one better. It will be snappier and all that. Beckerman: Do you think that translators have a kind of a compulsion to continue to play with the words, to work this Rubik’s Cube? Wilson: Yeah, I think it is compulsive, maybe that’s right, but I don’t think that’s just me. I think that is very common, especially for a very long project like this. You’re constantly thinking about, How might I be able to get closer to whatever the vision is? Read: A Trojan horse is supposed to have humans inside it. Beckerman: One of the things I love that you do in the book is demystify translation, and make us understand that these are human beings and that they have biases, that they’re in a specific cultural moment—a certain gender, a certain kind of life experience—and that it shows up in the word choices. When your translation of The Odyssey came out, a lot of the excitement about it was that you were the first woman who was translating The Odyssey in English. And I wonder if you feel like being a woman has affected your translation? Wilson: I think some of those headlines were kind of bogus, right? I mean, they were suggesting this really simplistic set of ideas about gender: And of course gender will be the only factor that will contribute to how someone writes. And of course all women write in iambic pentameter, just naturally, and all men don’t. Some of it was really silly, I thought. Beckerman: But do you feel like you understand the particular worldview that you’re bringing to it? None of us fully know the water we’re swimming in, of course, but when you’re translating, are you like, Oh, I think that I’m reading this way or I’m making these choices because I’m a woman of a certain age living in the 21st century in America? Wilson: You know, I honestly never think that. I mean, I think that when I’m talking to people like you. Well, I don’t mean “people like you”— Wilson: Exactly. It’s only very belatedly that I feel that I have to prepare an answer for things like this. When I’m actually wrestling with it, I do quite often think, Okay, I’m putting the stress on that, and that syllable and that name; is that because I’m British? To what extent is my approach to the passage informed by that critical study or this critical study? That kind of thing comes into my thinking all the time. But the question is about my lived experience, and do I have this particular relationship to this banquet scene because I don’t eat meat? No. Beckerman: Right. I mean, meter is important to you, that the sound of the poem or the text in another language have some relationship to the original. For translators 200 years ago, they wouldn’t necessarily care in the same way. Wilson: Yeah, this is a very old-fashioned choice on my part. I see myself as super retro. I see myself very much as taking a sort of mid-19th-century approach to translation. Beckerman: How do you think film adaptation is different or similar to literary translation? When you think about a director approaching a text like The Odyssey—wanting to adapt it and making very definite choices about the value system that is underlying the plot that he’s created—is that the same type of thinking through interpretation that a translator does when they approach a text? Wilson: I would say no. I think it’s a very different kind of thinking. What I’m trying to do as a translator is to get to something equivalent to the experience of the original. I think a good adaptation tries to do the opposite of that. Virgil was certainly doing that in rewriting The Odyssey as The Aeneid. It’s on some level the same story; it’s also the opposite story. So I think the more the adaptation pushes against it, in many cases the more successful it is artistically. Beckerman: There is this interesting sort of tension through a lot of these essays in your new book, where you want these ancient texts to be popular, to get out there, to have people engage with them and to make new things with them. But on the other hand, that often leads to a kind of a reductive quality. And I feel like it’s a tension that came out around Nolan’s movie. You wrote a very critical review of the film, and I had a moment as I read that when I wondered, Is she trying to distance herself from it as something that has reductive qualities, and is it popular in a way that doesn’t engage with the full texture of the text? Wilson: I wish I would have liked the film more. I went in hoping to like it and thinking I was going to like it. And it turns out I didn’t. It seems to me that criticism has a responsibility to tell the truth. There’s another kind of responsibility: that you shouldn’t actually lie about your actual feelings and thoughts. And there’s also a question about: What does it mean to spread the word that everyone should read ancient literature? Everyone should take some kind of interest in history. Everyone should engage with long, difficult books. I really believe in those things, and I don’t believe that the best way to engage with history and to grapple with the essential questions of art and cinema and plays and music and literature is to sort of say, Yay, we love it all. Beckerman: But what lenses did you put on to critique the film? As a translation or as an adaptation? Wilson: No. I mean, I say at the start of the review that very often the best adaptations are the ones that are most resistant to the original. The ways that I was critiquing the film were in terms of pacing and characterization and narrative. A satisfying cinematic experience needs to follow through on its own premises. And so if you set us up to think this is going to be the kind of film where we’re supposed to care about the characters, then you need to follow through on that promise. Read: An Odyssey deserving of the biggest screen possible Beckerman: What did you make of the response to your response—the backlash of sorts? I’m just curious what it told you about people’s relationship to these stories, because there was this real defensiveness about the film, about this effort to make a popular piece of art around The Odyssey. Were you surprised? Wilson: Yeah, I thought it was really weird. Many people reviewed this film, including other Odyssey translators, and there was no discourse about anyone except for me, which is really weird, I thought. So I’m not sure that I can really comment on what weird people thought. But I feel like this is taking us a bit far from the book? Because I don’t discuss the film in the book. I discuss theater and ancient literature. Beckerman: I know, I understand. It’s just that it was an interesting cultural moment. It is not every day that my Twitter feed is full of people wanting to debate the finer points of The Odyssey. So I can imagine, for somebody who’s as immersed in it as you are, that it must be unsettling but also exciting. Wilson: I think it’s wonderful insofar as it is actually substantially about either the film or about the poem, or some combination of the two. I felt that some of the discourse wasn’t about either of those things. I’m, on some level, interested in it because it’s a symptom of some kind of bad stuff in our culture, but I’m not that interested in it, and I’m not going to celebrate it. Beckerman: Right. So my last question: I’ve thought a lot about the limits to how much we can understand people’s lived experience so far in the past, and as a translator of these texts, you come the closest—you walk right up to that edge—and I’m curious for your thoughts about that. Is it never going to be possible for us to get at what it meant to be living in the time when The Iliad or The Odyssey were written? Or do you see a kind of universality that connects us to the people who wrote those texts? Wilson: That’s a great question and such a difficult one. I mean, part of my reason for focusing so much on literary form and poetic form is that even if we can’t get the lived, embodied experience of what it means to be going around in your chitons and eating your ancient Greek olives—I’m not sure that we can get to that through texts—I do think we can get quite close to something like the experience of being a fluent listener or reader. I don’t think that means universals, but I think it means we can get to a kind of multicultural imagination that includes going back in time as well as going to different places in space. And if we can do that, there’s a kind of magic in that in itself.

The Wisdom of Uncertainty
2026-09-19

The Wisdom of Uncertainty

The Atlantic | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
This is an edition of The Wonder Reader, a newsletter in which our editors recommend a set of stories to spark your curiosity and fill you with delight. Sign up here to get it every Saturday morning. How comfortable are you with saying, “I don’t know”? Most of us spend our lives trying to know more. We look things up, seek expert advice, and gather information before making decisions. In many cases when we disagree with someone, we assume that if we could just establish the facts, one of us would turn out to be right. We accumulate knowledge until, we hope, the uncertainty disappears. But what if uncertainty never fully goes away? In a recent Atlantic essay, the physicist Carlo Rovelli argues that modern science complicates the idea that we can ever arrive at a completely objective description of reality. Even physics suggests that what we see can depend on the relationship between the observer and what they’re looking at. We are not standing outside the world looking in, Rovelli writes. We are part of the world we are trying to understand. That doesn’t mean that facts don’t exist or that knowledge is useless. Rovelli points out that we make decisions without absolute certainty all the time, and science works much the same way: It can give us extraordinarily reliable knowledge without perfect certainty. The same idea can be freeing in everyday life: We can hold convictions without assuming that our understanding is complete, and approach other perspectives with curiosity rather than immediately dismissing them. Today’s newsletter explores the value of leaving room for uncertainty. Science will never be able to give a completely objective description of the world. That should make us humbler—and wiser. My quest to understand consciousness took me to a cave in New Mexico and then deep into the cosmos. The fundamental nature of living things challenges assumptions that physicists have held for centuries. (From 2025) My colleague Isabel Fattal recently asked readers to share a photo of something that sparks their sense of awe in the world. Nick G. shared a photo from “the volcanic blast zone of Mount St. Helens in Washington State during a summer sunrise.” We’ll continue to feature your responses in the coming weeks.

College Sports Are Great
2026-09-19

College Sports Are Great

The Atlantic | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
For a decade, I’ve been a member of a faculty committee that works closely with the athletics department at Northwestern University. A fellow professor once complained to me about the special admissions considerations that athletes receive, arguing that we could admit more oboists if athletes didn’t get so many spots. At the time, the university was being sued as part of athletics-department scandals involving sexual harassment and hazing. One case was dropped, and others were settled without the university admitting wrongdoing. During the same period, the university announced the construction of a brand-new football stadium that would be the most expensive ever built for a college team. It was a lot to digest all at once. In answer to my colleague, I nodded in agreement, thinking, Yeah, more oboists. But as I considered the mission of our university—helping students decide what they want to do after college, cultivating the knowledge and skills they need to achieve their goals, supporting faculty research, and raising capital to ensure that we can continue to do that work—I became less certain that oboists are more desirable, and more qualified for admission, than athletes. Why do we think that, over the course of their life, oboists will make a greater contribution to society than linebackers? (I mean you no offense, kind oboists! The duck is my favorite character in Peter and the Wolf.) On campus, many people remain doubtful that Northwestern’s athletics and academic missions are aligned, especially as football becomes more and more of a business sheltered by a university. Critics are right to lament the increased professionalization of college sports, but they also believe that most student athletes care more about their sport than their classes. Many academics see sports as frivolous and corrupting, as a distraction from the higher purpose of moral and intellectual formation—and as the most visible sign of the commercialization and corporatization of the modern university. We worry that out-of-control spending on sports could lead us to abandon our commitment to academic achievement. Sally Jenkins: The knives are out for Bill Belichick Many, maybe even most, of these negative views of athletics held by my colleague and others are based on misconceptions. Take the complaint that student athletes are paid. Other students have the opportunity to get paid for work they do for the university and on their own behalf. We have a start-up incubator that has raised more than $1.6 billion in capital. Why shouldn’t athletes profit from the work they do too? Our college athletes are hardly dumb jocks. As part of my work on the Committee on Athletics and Recreation (now called the Intercollegiate Athletics Committee), I’ve reviewed athletes’ selection of majors, academic performance, and travel schedules. I served on committees that hired our two most recent athletics directors. In my interactions with the athletics department and with student athletes in particular, I’ve been impressed, above all, by the emphasis they place on academic success, and how much effort they put into being students. I wish more of them wanted to major in my discipline, history, but that’s a different story. Despite the seemingly constant negative attention paid to youth sports, I’m not convinced that parents of athletes put any more pressure on their kids than the parents of kids involved in other pursuits do. Northwestern’s Division I athletes worked as hard to get here as the oboists—or dancers, debaters, and comedians—did. Their accomplishments are every bit as impressive, and their chosen path every bit as legitimate. Yet student athletes have told me that they sometimes feel like they don’t deserve to be here; that nonathletes don’t want to do group projects with them, either because they think that their practice and travel schedules will make them unreliable partners or that they are intellectual lightweights. In fact, athletes do as well as nonathletes in their classes, and they typically get better grades during their active season of play compared with the offseason. As the director of our American Studies program, I met frequently with majors, one of whom was a runner on our varsity cross-country team. In addition to talking about her thesis or the classes she needed to graduate, we talked about the discipline necessary for her to maintain her 3.9 GPA. Her answers combined behaviors she learned from sports and from academics: physical activity, nutrition, sleep, good study habits. I learned a lot from her about a life well lived. Student athletes do get support from tutors and advisers whom they’re required to meet with regularly. But nonathletes also have access to advisers, tutors, and other academic-support services. They aren’t required to use them, although it would benefit them if they did. Athletes do miss a lot of classes, which grates on professors. On average, they miss maybe five days of class per quarter. In a nine- or 10-week quarter, that could amount to 15 or 25 percent of all class meetings. When athletes arrive on campus, their advisers tell them they can major in anything they want. That’s not entirely true, because some majors have requirements, such as lab time in the natural sciences, that conflict with practice or competition schedules. I wish they really could major in anything, but all students and not just athletes decide which classes to take and which subjects to major in based on their other curricular and extracurricular commitments, including work and family obligations. They’re also the only students who have to announce at the beginning of each quarter how many classes they’ll skip. Football and, to a lesser degree, men’s basketball are fundamentally different from our 19 other varsity teams. These two sports spend and earn the majority of athletics-department revenue, are responsible for the most controversial changes to how college sports work, and receive almost all of the negative press. They make the system run and are inextricable from it. They, too, can add academic value. To be transparent, I’m a fan. I’ve traveled to football bowl games in Nashville and Las Vegas, and March Madness basketball games in Sacramento, where I met recent and older alumni.  To demonstrate how big-time college sports can help an entire university to prosper, a trustee pointed me to the example of Vanderbilt University, whose chancellor, Daniel Diermeier—previously on the faculty at Northwestern and the University of Chicago—has invested heavily in sports (especially football and basketball), written about the benefits to the university, and seen applications skyrocket and admissions rates dip. (There are also counterexamples; Northeastern University dropped its football program in 2009 and has still shot up the rankings.) Over the past decade or so, applications from high-school students in the Northeast to schools in the Southeastern Conference—the Big Ten’s primary rival in athletics, in terms of prestige and television dollars—have increased by more than 90 percent. Perhaps it’s a coincidence, but most SEC schools have climbed in the academic rankings over the same period. Maybe they’ve ridden the wave of social-media posts of outdoor parties in February, or Bama Rush. But maybe a good football team, along with fancy dorms and good food, is considered by many incoming students to be an essential part of university life. Meal plans and residence halls aren’t core to our academic mission, either. This fall, we’re debuting our new $875 million stadium. Our roster of college-football players will earn $21 million to $25 million to play this year, and our quarterback will make $1 million to $2 million. We’ve lured Chip Kelly to Evanston; as the former head coach of the NFL’s Philadelphia Eagles, he will be as much of a campus celebrity as an offensive coordinator can be. We’ve also landed a $35 million gift from the Uihlein family specifically for football. Mac Uihlein was a captain of the team last year. That donation has upset some of my peers not only because of the family’s politics—they’ve donated to pro–Donald Trump super PACs and national efforts to limit abortion rights—but also because it’s further evidence of our investment in sports at a moment of austerity for much of the university. It’s a big bet that spending so much on football will lead the team to victory and that competitive success will boost the whole university. The dramatic transformation of college sports makes it harder for me to justify my fandom—to myself and others. Conference expansion has shattered pretenses to regional coherence. Athletes now sometimes attend three, four, or five schools during their college career. Federal courts have held that student athletes can be paid directly by universities and simultaneously earn additional income from sponsorships and advertisers. Amateurism as the model on which college sports are based has always been a half truth, but today it’s basically dead. These facts are enough to make one wonder what the college half of college sports even means. As a faculty member, I’m fine with most of this, but with many reservations. When I taught a large lecture course on the history of college sports, I learned about a tackle named James Hogan, who played for Yale University from 1901 to 1904. He was in his mid-to-late 20s and didn’t pay tuition, and the university paid for his spring breaks in Havana. His story is a reminder that money has infected college sports for a long time. Even so, I’m concerned that the money our athletes receive will affect my interactions with them as students in my classes. Instead of learning history, will they be focused on how to invest or spend the dollars the athletics department is paying them? Their labor for the university is more valuable than mine, so will they wonder why they should have to listen to me at all? Yet I continue to believe that a university’s athletic and academic successes are complementary, if not connected. I still disagree with my colleague’s claim that sports drag down the more elevated intellectual and creative purpose of our institution. Football games and other sports connect generations of students, faculty, and alumni. Investments in athletics can boost a university’s academic reputation, along with our roster of poet laureates, Nobel Prize winners, and Marshall Scholars, and can also be a source of financial stability or even prosperity. Even now, I think college sports, on balance, do more good than harm. It has been a rough period at Northwestern and other institutions of higher learning. I see sports as part of what holds us together as a community at the same time that they’re one more source of tension for others, such as my oboist-loving colleague. Yes, there’s too much money in the system, and that money can invite corruption that goes against our values. But there’s also too little attention paid to what college athletes gain from a university education, and what the rest of the university gains from having first-rate athletes here, in the classroom as well as on the field. The value of big-time college sports is real—for players, for fans, for professors—although you do have to work harder than ever to see it.
The Atlantic
Trump Is Betting on the Wrong Europe
2026-09-19

Trump Is Betting on the Wrong Europe

The Atlantic | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
Donald Trump says he wants a Europe that is more capable of defending itself. But the nationalist-populist movements he is championing there will not advance this agenda and may put Europe at odds with the United States over China. Earlier this month, the nationalist-populist Alternative for Germany party came just a few seats short of an outright majority in a regional parliament, its best-ever result. This Sunday, Germans will vote in two more regional elections. The ruling Christian Democrats are behind in both races, and the AfD is expected to do well in one. On Wednesday, Chancellor Friedrich Merz canceled his visit to the United Nations General Assembly next week, sparking speculation that he could soon be forced to resign. U.S. officials are ebullient. Trump greeted the AfD’s recent electoral victory with a triumphant message on Truth Social that finished with “MGGA” (Make Germany Great Again). Richard Grenell, Trump’s former ambassador to Germany and now a presidential envoy, called the result “a huge win and a new day in Germany.” The Trump administration has consistently embraced Europe’s nationalist-populist parties as ideological partners, and even launched a State Department fund to support MAGA-aligned European groups. It surely sees the AfD’s ascendance as a sign that politics are moving in its direction. But that is true only if Trump’s real goal is a fragmented Europe that accommodates Russian and Chinese power and is unable to punch its weight. Such an outcome would be catastrophic for U.S. interests. Some people in the administration insist that the idea is not to weaken Europe but to shift the burden for its conventional defense to the Europeans themselves. Undersecretary of Defense for Policy Elbridge Colby and Secretary of State Marco Rubio, for example, reject claims that the administration is soft on Russia and say that they are calling instead for greater European self-sufficiency. Colby advocates for what he calls NATO 3.0, in which Europeans would take on the primary responsibility for their conventional defense. But the nationalist populists do not share this view. The AfD has called for an end to all arms deliveries to Ukraine and a lifting of all sanctions on Russia. Its co-leaders, Alice Weidel and Tino Chrupalla, boycotted Volodymyr Zelensky’s speech to the Bundestag in 2024, dismissing the Ukrainian president as a “war and beggar president.” In France, Marine Le Pen, the parliamentary leader of the nationalist-populist party National Rally and the front-runner for next spring’s presidential election, has promised to take France out of NATO’s integrated military command (France would remain a member of the alliance). Such a withdrawal would hardly help the Trump administration shift the burden of NATO’s defense to Europe. The Trump administration’s affinity for Europe’s nationalist-populist right seems to be almost entirely cultural and civilizational, and based on the notion that Europe’s populists are fighting the same culture wars—including against immigration—as their counterparts in the United States. J. D. Vance explicitly praised then–Hungarian Prime Minister Viktor Orbán as an ally in defense of Western civilization at an election rally in Budapest on the eve of the Hungarian elections. And the Trump team seems to see the European populist right’s enemies—the European Union and national political establishments—as versions of its own domestic foes. This commonality appears to take precedence over foreign-policy concerns. Trump officials have blasted centrist European governments for being too soft on Beijing but have said nothing about the populist right’s position on China. Some of these parties—particularly the AfD and Fidesz, in Hungary—favor much closer ties to the American adversary. The AfD’s Weidel lived in China for six years, speaks Mandarin, and wrote her doctoral dissertation about China’s pension system. Chrupalla, her co-leader, accused the German government of waging “economic war” against China for proposing a tougher trade policy toward Beijing. They have both called for warmer relations with China. Orbán, who was defeated in the Hungarian elections earlier this year, blocked the EU from criticizing Beijing, initiated an “all-weather comprehensive strategic partnership” with China, and welcomed Chinese investment in Hungarian infrastructure. These largely transactional attitudes toward China and Russia may reflect a broader worldview among the nationalist populists, who praise Trump but are deeply skeptical of American power. In an analysis for the Carnegie Endowment for International Peace, Jeremy Cliffe argued that the AfD supports a multipolar world order and believes that great powers, such as China and Russia, should possess spheres of influence—a perspective that Trump seems sympathetic toward. The AfD, Cliffe wrote, “may regard Trump as a civilizational ally, but its senior figures have long described the United States as a raumfremd in Europe, a hard to translate German word that means something like ‘territorially alien.’” Read: Europe’s far right is turning on Trump If parties with these views take power, they could reduce American influence in Europe while making common European action on Russia and China much more difficult. They will also make Eastern Europeans nervous about German rearmament. In 2011, Radek Sikorski, Poland’s foreign minister, said, “I fear German power less than German inaction.” He recently revisited that remark, telling the German newspaper Handelsblatt, “We want Germany, under democratic leadership and as the largest economy in Europe, to play a strong role in securing our common security. But we remember all too well where a rearmed Germany can lead in the hands of a far-right, pro-Russian party—and that is exactly what the AfD represents.” Senior Trump-administration officials say they are building a Europe that will carry its own weight. Maybe they are so beguiled by the nationalist populists’ domestic agenda that they have not yet realized it will come at the expense of NATO 3.0. Or maybe Trump doesn’t care about Europe’s defense, doesn’t see it as a U.S. interest, and doesn’t believe that Europe has any contribution to make to America’s security. Trump and Vance seem to be laying the foundation for a more divided Europe that is incapable of deterring Russia, ever more dependent on China, and at odds with itself. If the administration continues on this path, it may get the European culture war it wants, but the United States will lose its ally.
