[imgflip.com] The federal government has always (for at least several decades) used the threat of removing federal funding to force states to do things that they don't want to do. This is nothing new, and is not, as this summary implies, setting a dangerous precedent. The difference is that normally, this is used to do things that add standards intended to make things better/safer for the general public or serve some legitimate public need. This is being done to remove standards intended to protect the general public. As a general rule, states have always been allowed to have stricter laws than the federal government, just not more lax laws. Using the threat of removing federal funding to take away laws intended to protect the public's rights, reduce competition among content providers, and push the Internet more and more towards a content monopoly is doing the opposite of that. It's not the federal government using the threat of withdrawing funding to push its agenda that is bad. It is the agenda itself that is bad. It is fundamentally antithetical to the rule of law, fundamentally contrary to the regulatory powers required to maintain a functioning capitalist system, and fundamentally contrary to the public interest. It is putting greedy corporations ahead of the American people. Additionally, the executive branch taking such an action without authorization from Congress is also bad. The long history of doing this has, to my knowledge, been limited to the executive branch acting on laws passed by Congress that give them the authority to withhold funds for specific non-compliance reasons. Doing so in the absence of such laws is likely a violation of the separation of powers. But unfortunately, the current administration has a long history of violating that separation of powers, doing various illegal acts, knowing full well that it will take months for the courts to strike down those acts, and that the damage will have been done by then, and using the threat of such illegal executive orders as a way to extort concessions out of states, government agencies, corporations, nonprofits, and individuals. And that right there — the repeated willful commission of unconstitutional acts for borderline felonious purposes — is a fundamental abrogation of their oath of office. So the way I see it, there are only three ways to fix the problem: A. Pass laws clarifying that Congress has exclusive power of the purse, and that the executive branch shall not retract funding to any state, any agency, or any individual for any reason unless the right to retract funding for that specific reason is explicitly codified in the relevant law as an executive power, and that this rule shall apply to all government agencies, without exception, superseding all previously assumed authority, and providing criminal liability for anyone acting in contravention of this law, with no statute of limitations. That way, federal agency heads who act on such executive orders will no longer be protected by any assumption of legality or constitutionality, and will risk future criminal charges if they act on an executive order that violates the separation of powers in this way. B. Remove the people who are pushing this agenda, whether through the ballot box, through impeachment, or both. ...is this fucking legal to withhold federal funding to strong arm states into anti-consumer policies? Not quite. The safe c library is primarily about runtime memory safety checks which are a good thing. But rust is also about compile time enforcement of memory safety. This is not something you can get from a library for an existing language like C. That's not to say you couldn't make a C dialect that had rust-like guarantees. You can use non-standard extensions with C++ today to cover some of what Rust does, like -Wlifetime in C++ for something roughly equivalent to Rust's compile-time borrow checker. UBSan/ASan lets you enforce address and undefined behavior with runtime checks, it's useful and easy to use, but it has a big performance impact so you normally turn it off in production code. Google Carbon seems promising, but it deviates enough from C++ that it feels like yet another language. But theoretically you will be able to have a project that has a mix of C++ and Carbon and it should Just Work(tm). Ada / SPARK is an alternative to must of what Rust offers, it's a wildly different approach and not a 1:1 equivalent. But you can make reliable software and even do some formal verification and static analysis on it. It's easier to deal with violation messages from the Ada compiler than with Rust. But it's also a lot more work to put all the contracts and declarations in place (you need multiple files just to make a module, so it's a very different workflow compared to C/C++/Rust). An interesting alternative to C++ is: https://www.circle-lang.org/ [circle-lang.org]https://github.com/seanbaxter/... [github.com] - read the readme. The Theory Behind Government’s Cruelest Paperwork

The Theory Behind Government’s Cruelest Paperwork

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
Gabfest Reads is a monthly series from the hosts of Slate’s Political Gabfest podcast. This month, David Plotz talks with journalist Annie Lowrey about her new book The Time Tax: How the Government Wastes Our Time—and How to Fix It. In this excerpt, Lowrey explains the “ordeal mechanism theory,” the decades-old idea behind why some government benefits are designed to be difficult to access, and why she argues it ends up failing the people who need help most. This partial transcript has been edited and condensed for clarity. David Plotz: I had never heard of this concept in your book and I was like, ‘That’s an amazing concept.’ Can you just describe what the “ordeal mechanism theory” is? There’s some context in which I guess it could be right. But no, this turns out to be wrong because very often people who are very vulnerable, very low income just can’t make it through the ordeal. They don’t have phones, like up-to-date phones; they don’t have computers; they don’t have Wi-Fi; they don’t have stable addresses. Plus, their lives are really stressful, so it makes it really hard to get through things. But anyway, so that’s the ordeal mechanism theory.” Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. America Hates A.I. Donald Trump Loves It. What Is Going On Here?
2026-09-19

America Hates A.I. Donald Trump Loves It. What Is Going On Here?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
The Surge is your weekly political sanity check: What matters, what might, and what’s just political theater. Sign up here to get it in your inbox. Welcome to this week’s edition of the Surge, Slate’s politics newsletter that welcomes Mitch McConnell back to the Senate. Is Lindsey Graham next? If there was but one thing that happened this week, it was politics. New Hampshire, sulky and brooding, thinks it’s too “cool” to participate in a Democratic-wave election. Did Pete Hegseth almost get impeached this week? A Texas Republican candidate caused a proper foofaraw after getting deeply upset by a photo of brown people enjoying a football game. And please, we are still welcoming feedback on the changes around this newsletter. Let us know. But first: How much are these killer robots paying Donald Trump to like them? (Actually, that’s probably discoverable in public filings …) Last week’s calls from inside the house that A.I. companies are at risk of losing control of their monsters and killing everyone—true or not!—have continued to sweep the news. Congress rolled up its sleeves and got to work devising plans for slow-moving task forces that may or may not ever materialize, before leaving D.C. until November. The salience of A.I. concerns has skyrocketed overnight. Polls show deep concerns among the electorate about A.I. advances. This isn’t going to abate as an issue over time, either. With each passing day, A.I. will become a bigger and bigger part of everyone’s lives. Soon, you won’t be able to spit without hitting an A.I. agent coming at you with a knife. (Or, well, showing you a helpful recipe using ingredients you have sitting around the house.) In short, we’ve never seen a bigger layup issue for populists, left and right, heading down the stretch of an election season. Which makes it all the stranger that the guy who ushered in the new populist era, Donald Trump, doesn’t have it in him to say a cross word about these machines at all. He’ll only chastise those who do. “I am the Hoax Buster, and I’m right now breaking another Hoax—That AI is going to take over, consume, and destroy the World, and that Robots will be marching into our Cities, and getting rid of us all!” He posted this week. “This is even wilder than the RUSSIA, RUSSIA, RUSSIA HOAX, or the Global Warming Scam.” In a separate screed regarding the need to regulate A.I., Trump posted that the “only control or ‘guardrails’ that AI needs is a STRONG AND SMART (High IQ!) PRESIDENT, and the U.S.A. has that, in spades!” He called the A.I. warnings a “SICK conspiracy” and warned “Conspiracy Theorists, Treasonists, Traitors, and Leakers, BEWARE!” All of this follows a late-August post responding to the data center backlash when Trump chastised voters for their concerns. “If we kill the Golden Goose,” he wrote then, “you will only have yourselves to blame.” Why can’t Trump just ride the wave here, when he’s usually so good at co-opting shrieking public panics? A few thoughts come to mind. You could say he’s genuinely concerned about being outmaneuvered by China on A.I. and that this is informed by classified briefings he gets on the matter. But he just doodles pictures of hamburgers during those. More important to him is that the tech people are nice to him, always bringing him gifts and purchasing protection by donating to his political action committees. What’s also important to him is that A.I. buildout has been responsible for most, if not all, of the GDP growth under his administration. The “Golden Goose,” then, is the Trump economy’s paper gain. It’s that last part that’s so critical given Trump’s psychology: He views the attack on the engine of growth he presides over as an attack on him. Comparing the A.I. backlash to the “RUSSIA, RUSSIA, RUSSIA HOAX” is as close to a tell as there is. There haven’t been many bad polls for Democrats across the board since Labor Day, as pollsters shift to surveys of likely voters instead of registered voters and capture the Democratic-enthusiasm edge. (Required note of caution: Sometimes polls are wrong as Hell!) But there’s one race that’s standing out, much as the likeness of an old man’s face once did on Cannon Mountain. Some recent surveys show Democrat Chris Pappas and Republican John Sununu, who served in the Senate previously, locked in a tight contest in New Hampshire’s Senate race. And this week, the Cook Political Report shifted its race rating to “toss-up.” What’s going on here? Sure, New Hampshire’s not the bluest state in history, but it should be comfortably blue enough for a Democrat to win in this environment. And Pappas, a sitting congressman, should be a perfectly acceptable head of hair to complete the task. The most obvious issue is Pappas’ opponent’s last name. Sununus are one of New Hampshire’s royal families, and John’s brother, Chris, is a popular former governor. Republicans have also outspent Democrats in the race as they attempt something of a sneak attack, with plenty more coming in ad reservations this fall. Republican Gov. Kelly Ayotte, meanwhile, is cruising to reelection, and could carry Sununu along with her. We thiiiink this will sort itself out based on fundamentals, and that Republicans are mostly trying to get Democrats to spend money to defend it? But there are always anomalies in even would-be wave years, and voters might think the Senate candidate is his brother is the stuff from which anomalies are made. It’s not a situation that Democrats can ignore anymore, at least. California Gov. Gavin Newsom told CNN’s Jake Tapper while fly-fishing (?) that he would not run for president if Kamala Harris ran. He wasn’t even backed into this corner. This was just two of the fellas out there fishing, and Newsom said it with barely any prompt. “Why would I?” Newsom said, after Tapper responded with a huh? “I wouldn’t do that to her, I wouldn’t do that to you. I wouldn’t waste everyone’s time.” What that tells us is that he must believe, or know, that Harris won’t run. Because there’s no way that Newsom is not running for president. No way! He also told Tapper that he would have to check to make sure a run would be OK with his family, but they all say that—the family is always reportedly enthused (unseen, off-camera). Another possibility is that Newsom will do a sneaky li’l thing like quick-announcing his candidacy before she has a chance to announce hers. Because he’s clearly thought about how to beat her. When Tapper said that the argument against Harris would be that she’s had her shot, and lost, Newsom didn’t miss a beat. “Yeah,” Newsom said, “And I mean, that would be the approach to the campaign, certainly. That would be the case we’d make. A pretty good one, actually. That’s objectively true.” Watch out for these sneaky Californians, folks. They’re the Greatest Political Villains in America. Trump Can’t Stop Talking About Them. I Went Into Their Shadowy Lair.

They’re the Greatest Political Villains in America. Trump Can’t Stop Talking About Them. I Went Into Their Shadowy Lair.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
The facade of the building betrays no affiliation—neither hammer nor sickle. At 235 W. 23rd St. in Manhattan, the lobby is similarly spartan. All the signage is in black and white, the walls various shades of gray. A private equity firm resides on the fifth floor; an A.I. firm takes up the penthouse property on eight. The third and fourth floors have tenants as well. The directory says that on the seventh floor, there is the building’s management company, an education company, two publishers, and something else: the “Offices of the CPUSA”—that is, the headquarters of the Communist Party USA. On the occasion of America’s 250th anniversary, President Donald Trump took time away from the birthday revelry to warn of a grave and imposing adversary. “Communism is a mortal threat to American liberty. It is the greatest threat to our country, including World War I, World War II, Pearl Harbor, or even 9/11,” he said on July Fourth weekend this year, in South Dakota. That’s an awfully big threat, you might think, but the president is way ahead of you. “I think it’s the greatest threat that I’ve ever seen to our country,” he told a Georgia audience a few days before. In fact, according to the Atlantic, Trump mentioned communism or communist 15 times on that Georgia soapbox, “starting less than a minute into his hour-plus speech.” All summer long he’s been worked up, not over the antifa anarchists or the DSA socialists but over the commies in particular. He’s not alone. Secretary of State Marco Rubio is also riled by the Red hunt—he’s been convening politicians and law-enforcement officers and counterterrorism experts from across the globe to combat the “transnational threat.” And recently confirmed Secretary of Homeland Security Markwayne Mullin has seen the communists on our very own liberty-loving shores. He went on Fox News with a big-time scoop: He had identified the Communist Party logo on a “No Kings” rally flyer. “No Kings rally is literally sponsored by the Communist Party,” he ascertained. Red Scare politics are back, about that there is no question. But where, exactly, are those scary Reds who have infiltrated and organized and outflanked the administration? Certainly, it seems, they are everywhere. But individually, they are extremely difficult to find. They are not doing high-profile sabotage of corporations; they are not shooting it out in the coal mines. One place they definitely are, though, is in this building in Manhattan, right out there in the open for anyone to see. And so, on a late morning in late August, I buzzed the seventh floor and rode the elevator straight into the belly of the beast. In the middle of the CPUSA office, sitting at a table in a conference room with four walls of glass, was Joe Sims, the party’s co-chair, wearing a black newsboy cap. In front of him stood a walker. Across the table was another comrade, youthful office assistant Brooklyn Crawford. Usually, it would be slightly more bustling than this, Sims assured me. But, as it was late August, these were the only two people in the office, save for the odd delivery guy, dropping off a package. Already, this was a deeper trip into the inner sanctum than anyone in the Trump administration had managed. CPUSA co-chair Sims told me that despite the president’s pugilism, there had not yet been any Justice Department demands, nor congressional subpoenas, nor even right-wing agitators out front. “It’s not a crime to be a Communist in this country,” he said. Around the office were vestiges of times gone by. The walls were decorated with a framed cartoon of Vladimir Lenin* sweeping kings and capitalist fat cats from the Earth, and a charcoal drawing of a man with a mallet pointing to a bull’s-eye reading “$60,000,” part of a onetime fundraising drive. There was a stylized poster of Che Guevara celebrating a little-known holiday on Oct. 8—Day of the Heroic Guerrilla. Elsewhere, Soviet realist–style paintings hung. Gifts from Cuba, a plate from the Communist Party of Vietnam (given “with compliments”), and various statuettes and busts adorned the countertops and bookshelves. Framed photos of bygone leadership. There was a small kitchen, with a sign taped to the fridge: “Please clean up after yourself no maid service here!!!” Out of the back windows was a view of the Edge NYC, the flagship high-rise property of Hudson Yards, with its protuberant observation deck. Out of the front windows was the distinctive facade of the Chelsea Hotel. The office, Crawford told me, had been designed for transparency and decked out with sustainable materials: bamboo, linoleum tables made from linseed oil, recycled carpeting, and the glass-walled conference room, all in service of an environmentally aligned approach. I settled into a chair between comrade Sims and comrade Crawford and asked them about the party’s political program, the path toward revolution—a winding path, no doubt, but one that has required incredible endurance. The party has survived the Red Scare of the 1950s, the Cold War, and the Soviet collapse, and is somehow over 100 years old. And now it is readying another Red Scare. Let’s get this out of the way quickly: The USA Communist Party, contra Trump and Co., is not a major player in American politics these days, behind the scenes or out front. It works with local tenants’ groups, it boosts candidates, it piggybacks on the Democratic Socialists of America sometimes, but sometimes not. Its numbers have grown over the past decade, but not overwhelmingly. But arguably more important than the art collection or the political strategy, and perhaps more important even than the dues collection, in explaining the Communist Party’s unlikely longevity, is the building we were in itself. Because the CPUSA is not merely a tenant holding down an office on a surprisingly posh block of Manhattan’s very gilt-edged neighborhood of Chelsea. The CPUSA is the owner of the office. In fact, the Communist Party is owner of the entire building at 235 W. 23rd St., a flagship commercial property now valued at nearly $10 million. And one of the party’s essential functions at the moment, as it has been for the past 20 years, is something Trump could well understand: It is a commercial landlord, collecting rents from commercial tenants on the building’s other floors. The Communists and the president, those bitter adversaries, might have more in common than they think. But unlike Trump, the Communists really, really hate talking about it. The story of the CPUSA cannot be told without getting into the quirks and particularities of Gus Hall. In 1959 Hall was first elected secretary general of the party, a title he held until his death in 2000. Hall ran for president (of the United States) four times: twice in the 1970s, twice in the 1980s. His best finish came in 1976, when he netted just shy of 59,000 votes, though the exact count is contested. He was the genuine artifact, the son of an impoverished Minnesota miner, and he served as a lumberjack and a steelworker in his youth. Hall led the party through the exceedingly challenging times of the Cold War era, the aftermath of McCarthyism, and the subsequent collapse of the Soviet Union. In some sense, it is a miracle that the party survived, though it has held on in a deeply diminished fashion. Although Hall long maintained that the CPUSA was a fully independent entity, a Moscow newspaper reported in 1992 that Hall had been the recipient of $40 million in Soviet assistance between 1971 and 1990. “The struggle between those who own the wealth and those whose labor produces the wealth is one flaw in capitalism that will lead to socialism,” Hall said in 1996. And Hall took his own sermonizing to heart. In the late 1970s, during that period of Soviet patronage, Hall decided to get into real estate. He purchased the building on 23rd Street, according to public records, in the 1970s, taking out a $150,000 mortgage on the property at an 8 percent interest rate. That wasn’t his only shrewd money move. There were other properties Hall and the party owned too. Sims, meanwhile, told me that Hall also got the party into the stock market. Even in the 1970s, Hall had a reputation for “playing around with stocks,” Sims said. Under the leadership of Hall and national Chairman Henry Winston, the party raised capital funds from comrades, which were then invested. So while his politicking may have left something to be desired—the party bled membership while he celebrated North Korea and various Stalinist ideas—his asset accumulation proved visionary. “We have investments,” Sims told me, declining to further elaborate on the portfolio. For a long time, the CPUSA occupied the entire Chelsea building, despite a dwindling headcount. And when the 1907-built edifice required maintenance, some party members did not view it as a blessing. In a September 2001 report to the board, a year after Hall’s death, the party took stock of its holding. “Our building is a wonderful asset, a real treasure for our Party and the working class movement of which we are all immensely proud. It’s situated in a prime location, midtown Manhattan, worth million[s] in today’s market.” But having five-digit annual operating expenses “puts a tremendous burden on Party finances,” the report found. “You often hear that the building has a shabby appearance. Unfortunately we would have to admit this is all too true. The reality is we can’t keep on top of it. The building has been under capitalized for years.” Finally, the report resolves: Though the party “still [has] to be alert to the enemy,” the “decline in anti-communism has created new opportunities to utilize our building in a new way.” In other words: to lease it out. And so the revolutionaries became rent-seekers. Hall once famously said he was forced to buy his home in Yonkers—under his wife’s name, in 1960, for $21,000—because no landlord “would rent to a Communist leader.” Now the Communists were on the other side. By 2007, they were in the process of withdrawing from eight to four floors, after having spent $1 million on renovations, and renting the other four out. By 2026, they had vacated seven of eight. On the seventh floor, the new Red Square had not quite arrived. But it is worth being clear that this is not simply White House bluster or Fox News filler. Republicans in Congress, on the Ways and Means Committee, have subpoenaed various left-wing groups as part of an investigation into foreign funding linked to the Chinese Communist Party. But that dogged search for connections to the Chinese Communist Party has not led Republicans to the American Communist Party. “So far, thankfully, no,” Sims said. “As long as we pay our taxes, pay our electrical bill, I think we’re OK. “We have what we call fraternal ties with other Communist Parties, but we don’t have any economic relationships. Nor do these other groups, by the way.” When Vermont Sen. Bernie Sanders sparked the left-wing reawakening, in 2016, the CPUSA membership was nearly even with that of the fledgling Democratic Socialists of America, both sleepy holdovers of a bygone political moment. According to Gus Hall’s obituary in the New York Times in 2000, Communist Party enrollment “had dwindled to about 3,000, down from a peak of about 66,000 in 1939.” In 1962 a full 17 percent of the membership were reported to be federal informants. “Since 2016, we’ve recruited 20,000 people that have applied for membership in the party,” Sims told me, though the party has long suffered from overstatement when it comes to self-reported membership numbers. “I think about 4,000 people have joined since 2022,” he added, a more modest addendum. According to the party’s website, about a third of those pay dues. But even if that’s a generous figure, it’s paltry compared to the growth of the DSA over that same time period. In a decade, the group has gone from roughly 6,000 members to 120,000, marking some of its biggest gains in the past year or so. “I’m jealous,” Sims said. Critically, the DSA doesn’t just have members; it also has elected officials. At last count, there were six DSA members in the New York state Assembly, three DSA state senators, and four DSAers on the New York City Council. Bigger, perhaps, than all is the New York City mayor’s mansion, where democratic socialist Zohran Mamdani resides. And that’s just in New York: Soon, there will likely be DSA-affiliated members of Congress from Colorado, Pennsylvania, Michigan, and more. That is high on the to-do list for the CPUSA. “We got away from running candidates in the 1980s,” Sims told me. “That’s something we’d like to get back to.” “If we were to win an election, in a major city, or even in a midsize city … it would’ve been on the front page of the New York Times. It’s one thing for a Socialist to win, but for a Communist, oh my God,” he said. Running candidates is not the only area in which the group differs with the DSA, despite the shared affinity for the color red. On some issues there is agreement, and the two groups work in coalition. “Not an ideological configuration but a practical one,” said Sims. DSA groups sometimes come and use the office when there is strategic alignment and a need for a venue. But not always. “On some things we don’t agree, like on housing. We’re for public housing. They’re for a scheme which goes in the direction of privatization,” said Sims, referring to RAD/PACT, a policy supported by the mayor that would see Section 9, or traditional public housing, converted to Section 8, which the city owns but doesn’t directly fund or manage. Another point of difference would be the property itself. While property ownership has been a controversy-causer in the DSA of late—the New York Post has sought, perhaps effectively, to gin up outrage over DSA co-Chair Gustavo Gordillo’s ownership of a Brooklyn home purchased by his parents—the CPUSA is not losing sleep over implications of hypocrisy with regard to its own property ownership. “People outside of leftist spaces will be like, ‘How do you call yourself a Communist—you have an iPhone?’ We all live in a society where we’re trying to live and survive and participate,” said Crawford. For now, the party sees the Republican Party in a creep toward fascism, and forestalling that is a top priority. As such, the Communists remain interested in helping Democrats win elections. That means voter turnout, as well as coalition-building on certain issues. “We’re not so much interested in a Democratic victory for its own sake, but we’re for pushing back that danger as much as possible. We live in the real world, and in the real world there are differences between the two parties on issues that affect working people,” Sims said. A Democratic majority in the House would, among other things, probably cool off the Red Scare redux. “The Democratic Party, though it has a lot of weaknesses, they haven’t joined the choir,” he said. Flipping the House to a Democratic majority would get the gavel away from Republican Ways and Means Committee Chair Jason Smith and put an end to his Joe McCarthy impression. This office, though quiet on this Thursday, does get used—it is not just “a museum of history,” as Gus Hall used to call it. The Young Communist League meets there; some tenant groups from local public housing units will gather there as well. The party continues to publish People’s World, the publication once (and perhaps better) known as the Daily Worker. Is the publication funded primarily by rents raised from the office building? I asked. “You keep coming back to the building,” Sims said. “No.” It’s true—I kept coming back to the building. I couldn’t help it. A website mocked up by commercial real estate brokerage RDE Advisors celebrates its many advantages. “12’ ceilings throughout, with a spectacular 16’ ceiling penthouse on the 8th floor,” it reads. “What could 235 W. 23rd become?” asks another part of the site. “AI Tech Headquarters” and “Fashion Agency” are among the suggestions. The building boasts 55,362 square feet of rentable space, roughly 6,766 per floor. The site claims that all of the floors are vacant/available or soon will be, including the Communist Party’s seventh, which is not correct. The asking price—$39 per square foot, per year—looked like a bargain when compared cursorily to other available office space in the neighborhood listed online. “This is one of the few remaining opportunities in Manhattan to consolidate your entire organization under one roof,” the site says. “The landlord will consider leasing floors individually.” Crawford, the assistant, clarified to me that they would rent to most people, “though we wouldn’t rent to Turning Point USA.” The leasing agents didn’t respond to my requests for comment. I reached out to all of the tenants as well, to ask what it was like to have the Communist Party as a landlord. Only Ross Cameron, co-founder of Lyric Capital Group, a private equity firm focused on music royalties, housed on four, responded. “This email made me laugh out loud! Didn’t know there was a connection with the owner to the Communist Party and honestly didn’t know there was still an American Communist Party!” he said. Agentic security is the billion-dollar challenge for some clever startup to solve
2026-09-19

Agentic security is the billion-dollar challenge for some clever startup to solve

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
High time to stop kicking the security can down the road, investor tells The Reg When it comes to AI models, security functions as an afterthought, as evidenced by increased instances of agents hacking organizations and people, and other security mishaps with agents gone rogue. There's also an opportunity here for companies to offer new solutions. This should not come as a shock to anyone, according to cybersecurity investors and accelerator executives. “On one hand, we shouldn’t be surprised that increasingly capable agents are finding creative and sometimes unexpected ways to accomplish their objectives,” Matt Hartman, chief strategy officer at Merlin Group, told The Register. “On the other, we can’t accept harmful behavior as inevitable or unmanageable.” It’s the same story that plays out with every emerging technology, from laptops to cloud, said Todd Graham, managing partner at Microsoft’s M12 venture fund. “Every time we've built a new piece of infrastructure, we've conveniently forgotten the security,” Graham told The Register. Herein lies the opportunity for early-stage security companies. “If laptops were default secure, we wouldn't have CrowdStrike,” Graham said. “If the cloud was default secure, we wouldn't have Wiz. If identity wasn't default secure, we wouldn't have a bunch of Active Directory add-ons and Okta.” When it comes to AI security, “a lot of ships are going to rise with this tide,” he added. What’s different with AI is the speed at which models are advancing. While companies adopted cloud technologies over a period of years, organizations are moving full speed ahead to incorporate agents and other AI tools into their production environments and allow them to access the most business-critical data and applications. Yet they don’t have a strong handle on how to manage, secure, or even identify the agents that are already roving about their systems. “This is a transition happening month over month, and given the rate of change we’re seeing in the market, I’m in no way or shape surprised that this issue has come to a head in a rather dramatic fashion,” Graham said. “The incidents that have occurred - I’m not going to defend them, but they should be a wake up call. This is a moment in time where we need to insert security, and we're just going to have to insert it faster.” Hartman joined Merlin after a lengthy career in federal cybersecurity, including senior roles at the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency. In his private-sector role, he helps determine which early- to growth-stage cybersecurity companies the group invests in, and then works with these firms to scale their technology across government, critical infrastructure, and other highly regulated markets. “We’re particularly interested in the security layer that governs agent behavior: identity for non-human actors, clear limits on what they can access and do, and an audit trail for actions taken on an agency’s behalf,” he said. “Agencies aren’t just asking how to adopt agents, they’re asking how to constrain them and prove what one did at 2 AM on a Tuesday.” But while “the opportunity is enormous,” startup founders need to do more than just build an agentic AI security product. “AI has made it faster and cheaper than ever to build a product, so the bar for differentiation keeps rising,” Hartman said. “As the cost of building technology falls, the value shifts toward differentiated capabilities and the ability to take them to market. Founders who can do both have a real opportunity to define this category.” Graham also says that end-users are looking for agentic identity and governance products - “I truly believe someone is going to build the next Okta, just as SaaS generated Okta,” - but adds he sees several founders “thinking way too small.” “I’m seeing a lot of companies that are solving a sliver of the problem,” Graham said. “And the reality is, if I'm a CISO for a Fortune 500 company, no way I'm going to go buy 15 things to do one thing. If you look at the standard identity stack that we’ve had for humans for quite some time, it has governance, you know, access control, authorization, access. For agents, someone's going to have to come to us with a solution that does all of the things.” When it comes to non-human identities, however, that’s a really big, tough ask. Service accounts remain a prime target for hackers because they typically have high privileges and passwords that never expire. Securing these non-human accounts still plagues security teams - and that’s even before agents entered the mix. Beyond agentic identity, AI endpoint security - think of this as CrowdStrike for AI - is another area ripe for inventive startups, Graham said. He says it’s a challenge he would personally tackle as a founder, but he’s been banned from starting any more companies, so that’s not going to happen. “If you have a breach, and you know you get hauled in front of Congress to explain why you didn't have antivirus turned on [or] EDR, you’re going to add AI endpoint pretty quickly to that list,” Graham said. “It feels like a very hot area that is still early enough, if someone wanted to build a quality solution.” Existing endpoint and antivirus vendors “will absolutely” build products to fill this void, he added. “But it does feel like again a moment in time where disruption is coming for everyone, especially those that have you know pre-existing commitments to go solve.” Graham admits he is “worried” about the recent real-world bad behavior by AI agents. “If left unencumbered, if left to its own devices, I am very concerned about where the endpoint is for this,” he said. Still, he’s “comforted” because he’s seen this scenario play out before, with security scrambling to keep up with infrastructure development. Graham also was "pleasantly surprised” by Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei’s now infamous “We Must Pace the Frontier” essay. “I'm not taking the cynical view that it's some sort of grand conspiracy to get around antitrust,” he told us. “I'm a believer that AI is this awesome tool that is going to fundamentally change a lot of lives for the better. But we’ve got to put in the work now,” Graham said. “We've kicked the security can down the road long enough, and now we need to solve it.” ® High time to stop kicking the security can down the road, investor tells The Reg Report says estimates focused on GPUs miss the mountains of power, cooling, and networking gear destined for scrap PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity From Waterfox to LibreWolf and Pale Moon, there's a browser for almost every Mozilla dissenter Downloading a model is increasingly easy. AI boom could leave an e-waste trail that wraps 6 times around Earth
2026-09-19

AI boom could leave an e-waste trail that wraps 6 times around Earth

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
Report says estimates focused on GPUs miss the mountains of power, cooling, and networking gear destined for scrap AI infrastructure could generate enough electronic waste by 2050 to fill a line of shipping containers stretching around Earth six times, according to a report that argues existing estimates drastically understate the problem. Previous estimates have focused chiefly on servers and accelerators such as GPUs, which account for just 13 percent of a datacenter's equipment by weight, the report says. Once power, networking, cooling, and other infrastructure are included, the total could be 40 to 60 times higher than the most widely cited academic projections. The report identifies five equipment categories comprising networking, power distribution, backup power systems, servers plus accelerators, and cooling. Together, these add up to about 7,000 metric tons for a reference 100 MW AI bit barn, much of which will have to be replaced when the facility is upgraded, it argues. The report, How Big Is the AI Waste Wave? [PDF], comes from the Basel Action Network (BAN), a nonprofit organization named after the Basel Convention, which controls international movements of hazardous waste and seeks to prevent its transfer from developed to developing countries. BAN's model starts with what the industry says it intends to build and calculates the potential waste implied by that expansion. It estimates that AI infrastructure will generate between 395 million and 617 million tonnes of e-waste from 2025 to 2050. The report says that would fill between 15 million and 23 million shipping containers. Placing 20 million of them end to end would create a line about 244,000 km long, or roughly six times Earth's circumference. The estimate rests on several assumptions. The model supposes that the copper, steel, cooling and power distribution equipment, networking gear, and computing hardware in the latest AI datacenters will be decommissioned within years rather than decades as the technology becomes obsolete. For example, the report says that when a facility replaces racks drawing between 5 and 15 kW with the 50-140 kW racks required for AI workloads, much of its power infrastructure must be ripped out and replaced. A single 100 MW facility contains about 2,700 tonnes of copper in its cabling and busbars alone. GPUs and other AI accelerators are replaced every two to three years, BAN claims, compared with five to seven years for traditional general-purpose servers. The model assumes that owners replace entire servers when upgrading to the latest generation of Nvidia GPUs, for example. BAN argues this is plausible because the OEMs supplying hyperscalers tend to ship complete, preconfigured systems. Other equipment has different replacement cycles: three to four years for networking infrastructure, eight years for power distribution, and five years for both backup power and cooling systems. The 46-page paper is the first in a series. The second will examine how reuse, refurbishment, and repurposing could reduce the volume of e-waste. The third will consider its potential toxicity, including contamination by perfluoroalkyl and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS), while the fourth will examine ways to mitigate the risks. No hyperscaler, government, or international body has published a plan for handling AI-driven e-waste on the scale projected, the report says. It adds that there is insufficient infrastructure to process even current volumes safely. "To date the environmental debate around AI has focused on electricity, carbon and water while largely overlooking what happens to the hardware itself," said BAN founder and chief of strategic direction Jim Puckett. "If companies and governments do not begin planning for this new waste tsunami, today's AI buildout could become an even more cataclysmic toxic waste crisis than we are already experiencing." ® High time to stop kicking the security can down the road, investor tells The Reg Report says estimates focused on GPUs miss the mountains of power, cooling, and networking gear destined for scrap PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity From Waterfox to LibreWolf and Pale Moon, there's a browser for almost every Mozilla dissenter Downloading a model is increasingly easy. Virginia governor wakes up to fact datacenters have become political cancer
2026-09-18

Virginia governor wakes up to fact datacenters have become political cancer

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Executive order puts the kibosh on NDAs, limits permitting, and calls for stiffer environmental protections Virginia is not just for lovers, but is also for datacenters, hosting more than anywhere else in the world. However, the growth could be slowing as Governor Abigail Spanberger issued an executive order on Friday cracking down on bit barn builds in the state. “In the years before I took office, datacenters came to Virginia and the Commonwealth did not have a clear or coordinated plan to address their impacts on Virginians … That changes today,” Spanberger said in a statement. The executive order is part of a broader datacenter accountability framework that calls for greater transparency, a ban on nondisclosure agreements, limits on permitting, and more stringent environmental controls. Spanberger argues that existing policy lacked the precision necessary to hold bit barn builders accountable, and that the order is necessary to protect residents. In addition to greater transparency, the broader framework would require local approval for datacenters exceeding 25 megawatts and remove future large datacenters from the expedited permitting process, while the order calls on government officials to establish criteria to distinguish between responsible datacenter developments and those that would negatively impact residents or the environment. Speaking of the environment, the order would limit the use of water-intensive cooling towers in areas suffering from water scarcity, and encourage datacenter operators to either retrofit existing backup generators to reduce emissions or switch to battery storage, fuel cells, and other clean energy sources. Friday's order comes just days after the Loudoun County Board of Supervisors backed a plan to pause final action on certain new datacenter and substation applications for up to 12 months. Loudoun County is home to one of the highest concentrations of datacenters in the state. Opposition to datacenter development across the US is on the rise amid growing concerns over the server farms' impact on air quality, noise pollution, water use, and residents' electric bills. In Tennessee, Elon Musk's Colossus 2 datacenter is powered in part by 27 gas turbines across the state line in Mississippi, which are now the subject of an NAACP lawsuit alleging violations of the Clean Air Act. Meanwhile, some states have gone even further and outright blocked the development of new datacenters while local governments get a better handle on their potential impacts. In July, New York became the first state to temporarily halt datacenter buildouts exceeding 50 megawatts as part of an executive order signed by Governor Kathy Hochul. A month later, Pennsylvania Governor Josh Shapiro issued an executive order blocking datacenter permit applications unless they made legally binding commitments to abide by the Governor’s Responsible Infrastructure Development (GRID) requirements. Much like Virginia’s executive order, these rules seek to ensure residents don’t end up footing the bill for grid improvements built based on speculative demand. ® High time to stop kicking the security can down the road, investor tells The Reg Report says estimates focused on GPUs miss the mountains of power, cooling, and networking gear destined for scrap PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity From Waterfox to LibreWolf and Pale Moon, there's a browser for almost every Mozilla dissenter Downloading a model is increasingly easy. Gemini went rogue, hacked three companies, and Google hid it
2026-09-19

Gemini went rogue, hacked three companies, and Google hid it

The Verge | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
Google says that breaking containment and targeting real companies doesn’t constitute ‘misalignment.’ Google says that breaking containment and targeting real companies doesn’t constitute ‘misalignment.’ In May, Gemini broke containment and hacked three different companies, but Google didn’t disclose the incident until the Wall Street Journal approached the company. The hacks happened during a test of the model’s cybersecurity capabilities run by third-party Irregular, which was also involved in similar incidents involving Meta and OpenAI. According to WSJ, Google didn’t disclose the hack because it didn’t consider it to be an “example of model misalignment.” The company said that it was an instance of “mistaken identity,” and once the model realized it had brute-forced its way into a real company by guessing a password, it stopped. “In this case, the model acted appropriately,” Google VP of Security Engineering Heather Adkins said. Adkins told The Verge that “the model found public information online and guessed credentials to access websites it thought were part of the test. In all three of these instances, the model stopped.” Adkins didn’t elaborate on how Gemini taking it upon itself to break containment and target third parties failed to qualify as misalignment. “Our security team has a long track record of reporting issues we find in other people’s software and systems - even if it’s as simple as a weak password,” she said. “We ensured the three entities were made aware, and we worked with our training partner on the changes they’ve now made to their testing processes. These events highlight the importance of training powerful AI models to act responsibly.” But Jack Cable, CEO of AI security firm Corridor, told WSJ that, “the meta problem is, hey, models are going outside the bounds of what they should be doing, and doing actual cyberattacks.” Additionally, security lapses at Irregular may have made these attacks possible. The model wasn’t supposed to have internet access during testing, but Irregular told WSJ it was unintentionally left available. As incidents like this pile up, calls to rein in AI have only grown. The chiptune rock band formed in 2004 on forums and remains very online to this day. The chiptune rock band formed in 2004 on forums and remains very online to this day. Anamanaguchi, the band consisting of Peter Berkman, James DeVito, Luke Silas, and Ary Warnaar, are most known for their chiptune music. Like me, you might have first heard them in game soundtracks like 2010’s Scott Pilgrim vs. the World: The Game or know of their collaboration with Hatsune Miku (which even made its way into Fortnite). They’ve gone through different eras of breakneck chiptune beats and dance-y pop songs, but their 2025 album Anyway took an indie rock departure — recorded in person at the American Football house and filled with fuzzy guitars. They’re also a band that’s terminally online and very plugged into the video game scene as well as the weirder sides of the internet — just listen to Peter and Luke’s guest spot on the Panic World podcast talking about Etsy witches. Anamanaguchi has a new single, “Yeah I Guess,” continuing in the musical direction of Anyway. And if it’s a precursor to a new album dropping soon, I’m sure I’ll be looping it endlessly just as I did the tracks “Magnet,” “Fall Away,” and “Valley of Silence.” (Seriously, I’ve at times had Anyway set on repeat for hours straight.) Not only did all four members of Anamanaguchi agree to answer our weekly questionnaire, but they answered all our questions. Luke Silas: I think my most used tool is Ableton Live Operator, it is simple and powerful and can make basic things happen incredibly quickly. I usually open it up just for writing or getting ideas down, but more often than not the sounds I get with it tend to make it to the final product! Ary Warnaar: Built-in laptop mic and cellphone mic aren’t exactly slept on, but def underappreciated. They’re great for just getting ideas down fast, but now they sound so good that I find myself not even re-recording what I thought would be “scratch” vocals. Peter Berkman: Right now it’s the LOGITECH MX MASTER 3S. Best mouse I’ve ever had. It has a sideways scroll. It has a button to switch between notched and smooth scroll wheel. The click is satisfying but still really quiet. It’s really sensitive & responsive on whatever surface I happen to have it on. It feels really good in my hand. It charges directly into a USB-C port without external batteries so it gets to look like a mouse with a tail after it’s worked really hard. When I’m at a different computer, or if I’m on a laptop having to use a trackpad, I feel like I’ve lost so much gestural fidelity. I love the keyboard but let’s be real Douglas Engelbart cooked when he invented the mouse. But he didn’t have the recipe of the LOGITECH MX MASTER 3S and he didn’t even live to see it. The LOGITECH MX MASTER 3S wasn’t even invented until June 2022. I have seen two friends recently using the LOGITECH MX MASTER 3S and I say “Hey man that’s the LOGITECH MX MASTER 3S” I use that thing too. James DeVito: Lately it’s been an app called “Amphetamine” that keeps your computer from going to sleep for a set amount of time.
